[^1019]: Troparion of the Prophecy at the Sixth Hour of Wednesday of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast, Tone 1.
[^1020]: Sixth Hour first Prokeimenon (Psalm 91:2, 3 [LXX 91:2, 3], Tone 4).
[^1021]: Isaiah 41:4–14 (LXX), the prophecy at the Sixth Hour of Wednesday of the Fifth Week, contrasting the folly and vanity of idol-makers with the eternal God Who upholds Israel His chosen servant.
[^1022]: Sixth Hour second Prokeimenon (Psalm 92:1, 1b [LXX 92:1, 1b], Tone 6).
[^1023]: Ninth Hour Kathisma: Kathisma 6 (Psalms 37–45 [LXX 37–45]).
[^1024]: Wednesday evening of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast (Vespers), which commences the great liturgical eve of the Standing of the Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete.
[^1025]: In the Great Fast, Kathisma 7 of the Psalter (Psalms 46–54 [LXX 46–54]) is chanted at Wednesday Vespers.
[^1026]: Wednesday Vespers Idiomelon, Tone 8, chanted twice, continuing the contemplation of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10:30–37).
[^1027]: Martyrikon of Tone 8.
[^1028]: Vespers Stichera of the Triodion by St. Joseph the Hymnographer, Tone 8, Podoben: "O Lord, though before the judgment seat" (Гдⷭ҇и, а҆́ще и҆ на сꙋди́щи / Κύριε, εἰ καὶ κριτηρίῳ).
[^1029]: Vespers Sticheron by St. Theodore the Studite, Tone 8, Podoben: "The glorious and most pure" (Сла́внꙋю и҆ Пречⷭ҇тꙋю / Τὴν ἔνδοξον καὶ ἄχранτον).
[^1030]: The twenty-four alphabetical penitential stichera by Saint Andrew of Crete (Tone 4, Podoben: "I desired with tears" [Хотѣ́хъ слеза́ми / Ἤθελον δάκρυσιν]), appointed for Wednesday Vespers of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast as the prelude to the Great Canon chanted at Thursday Matins. In the Greek original, each sticheron forms an acrostic following the twenty-four letters of the Greek alphabet from Alpha (Ἅπαντα τὸν βίον μου) to Omega (Ὦ τῆς ἀφάτου σου εὐσπλαγχνίας). In the Slavonic printed Triodion, the printer preserves this sequence by numbering the stichera in the margin with Cyrillic numerical letters (А҃ through Ѡ҃), with sticheron 15 corresponding to Greek Omicron.
[^1031]: Rubric directing three full prostrations (metanias) to be made at each of the twenty-four alphabetical stichera, totaling seventy-two prostrations at "Lord, I have cried."
[^1032]: First Vespers Prokeimenon (Psalm 93:1, 2 [LXX 93:1, 2], Tone 4).
[^1033]: Genesis 17:1–9 (LXX), recording God's covenant with Abram at age ninety-nine, the change of his name to Abraham, and the promise that he will be the father of many nations and inherit the land of Canaan.
[^1034]: Second Vespers Prokeimenon (Psalm 94:1, 2 [LXX 94:1, 2], Tone 4).
[^1035]: Proverbs 15:20–16:9 (LXX), appointed for the second reading of Wednesday Vespers in the Fifth Week of the Great Fast.
[^1036]: The Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts celebrated on Wednesday evening of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast.
[^1037]: Rubric for when the Presanctified Liturgy is not celebrated; the 24 alphabetical stichera of the Great Canon by St. Andrew of Crete are sung at Vespers.
[^1038]: Trapeza rubric permitting wine and oil on Wednesday of the Fifth Week of Great Lent in anticipation of the spiritual and physical labor of the great All-Night Vigil of the Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete.
[^1039]: Compline and Midnight Office rubrics prescribing the chanting of the Kontakion of the Great Canon of St. Andrew ("My soul, my soul, arise...") after the Trisagion prayers.
[^1040]: Thursday of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast, commonly known as the Thursday of the Great Canon (or the Standing of St. Andrew of Crete), celebrated with Matins at which the entire Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete is chanted in full alongside the Life of St. Mary of Egypt.
[^1041]: The striking of the sounding board (semantron/klepalo) at the 4th hour of the night corresponds traditionally to about 10:00 PM or early night hours in monastic usage, signaling the commencement of the solemn All-Night Vigil.
[^1042]: Kathisma 8 of the Psalter (Psalms 55–63 [LXX 55–63]), appointed as the single Kathisma at this service, in order to allow sufficient time for the singing of the entire Great Canon and the reading of the Life of St. Mary of Egypt.
[^1043]: The reading of the Life of St. Mary of Egypt written by St. Sophronius, Patriarch of Jerusalem, divided into two sections (staseis): the first part read here after the Kathisma, and the second part after Ode 6 of the Great Canon.
[^1044]: The biblical canticles (odes) are omitted from their usual chanting because of the immense length of the 250 troparia of the Great Canon.
[^1045]: Rubric directing three full metanias (prostrations to the ground) to be made at each troparion of the Great Canon.
[^1046]: St. Andrew of Crete (c. 660–740), born in Damascus, monk of the Brotherhood of the Holy Sepulchre in Jerusalem (hence called "of Jerusalem"), later Archbishop of Gortyna in Crete, composer of the Great Penitential Canon.
[^1047]: Ode 1 Heirmos of the Great Canon, Tone 6: "A Helper and Protector" (Помо́щникъ и҆ покрови́тель / Βοηθὸς καὶ σκεπαστής), drawing upon Exodus 15:1–2.
[^1048]: The penitential refrain chanted before each troparion of the Great Canon: "Glory to Thee, our God, glory to Thee" (Слава тебѣ Боже нашъ, слава тебѣ).
[^1049]: The spiritual allegory of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10:30–37) and the Parable of the Prodigal Son (Luke 15:11–32) interwoven with the Old Testament types of Adam, Eve, Cain, and Abel in the opening ode of the Great Canon.
[^1050]: St. Andrew of Crete draws directly upon the Parable of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10:30–37) and Psalm 142:2 [LXX 142:2: "Enter not into judgment with Thy servant"].
[^1051]: The Canon to Venerable Mary of Egypt by St. Theophanes the Branded (or Clement), added to the Great Canon at each ode, chanted in Tone 6 with the same heirmoi.
[^1052]: The acrostic in the Greek Triodion: Σὺ ὁσία Μαρία βοήθει ("Do thou, Venerable Mary, help").
[^1053]: Refrain for the troparia of Venerable Mary of Egypt: "Venerable Mother Mary, pray to God for us" (Преподобная мати Марїе, моли Бога о насъ).
[^1054]: Refrain for the troparia added in honor of St. Andrew of Crete: "Venerable Father Andrew, pray to God for us" (Преподобне отче Андрее, моли Бога о насъ).
[^1055]: Ode 2 Heirmos of the Great Canon, Tone 6: "Give ear, O heaven, and I will speak" (Вонми небо, и возглаголю / Πρόсехе οὐρανέ, καὶ λαλήσω), drawing upon the Great Canticle of Moses in Deuteronomy 32:1.
[^1056]: St. Andrew meditates on the coats of skins and fig leaves from Genesis 3:7, 21, Psalm 128:3 [LXX 128:3: "The sinners wrought upon my back"], and the Parable of the Lost Coin (Luke 15:8–10).
[^1057]: Ode 2 contains two distinct heirmoi and sections in the Great Canon. This second Heirmos of Ode 2 ("See, see that I am God" / Ἴδετε ἴδετε, ὅτι ἐγώ εἰμι Θεός) is drawn from Deuteronomy 32:39, and Exodus 16–17.
[^1058]: St. Andrew contrasts the penitent's fall with the antediluvian patriarchs: Seth (Genesis 4:25), Enos who began to call upon the name of the Lord (Genesis 4:26), Enoch who pleased God and was translated (Genesis 5:24), and Noah who built the ark (Genesis 6–7).
[^1059]: Genesis 4:23 (the song of Lamech), Genesis 11:1–9 (the Tower of Babel), and Genesis 19:24 (the destruction of Sodom).
[^1060]: Ode 3 Heirmos of the Great Canon, Tone 6: "Upon the immovable rock" (На недвижимомъ, Христе, камени / Ἐν πέτρᾳ με τῆς πίστεως στερεώσας), based upon 1 Kingdoms 2:2 [1 Samuel 2:2] and 1 Corinthians 10:4.
[^1061]: The flight of Lot to the mountain and Zoar (Genesis 19:17–23) presented as an urgent moral exhortation to escape the fiery destruction of passions.
[^1062]: Second Heirmos of Ode 3 of the Great Canon, Tone 6: "Establish, O Lord, upon the rock" (Утверди Господи, на камени / Στερέωσον Κύριε ἐπὶ τὴν πέτραν), based upon 1 Kingdoms 2:2 [1 Samuel 2:2].
[^1063]: The antediluvian and patriarchal exemplars: Noah's contemporaries (Genesis 6), Ham uncovered his father's nakedness (Genesis 9:22), Shem and Japhs blessed (Genesis 9:26–27), Abraham called out of Haran (Genesis 12:1–4) and entertaining angels at Mamre (Genesis 18), Isaac offered on Moriah (Genesis 22), Ishmael and Hagar cast out (Genesis 21), Jacob's ladder (Genesis 28:12), Melchizedek king of Salem and priest of the Most High God (Genesis 14:18; Hebrews 7:1–3), and Lot's wife turned into a pillar of salt (Genesis 19:26).
[^1064]: Sessional Hymn (Kathisma) by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), Tone 8, chanted after Ode 3. The hymn addresses the Twelve Apostles as radiant luminaries guiding believers through the nocturnal darkness of passions toward the joy of Christ's Holy Passion.
[^1065]: Second Sessional Hymn to the Holy Apostles, Tone 8, Podoben: "That which was commanded mystically" (Повелѣ́нное та́инственно / Τὸ προσταχθὲν μυστικῶς), imploring the apostolic college to intercede for the faithful during the spiritual course of the Fast.
[^1066]: Theotokion concluding the sessional hymns after Ode 3, invoking the intercession of the Mother of God together with the Holy Apostles for peace throughout the world and repentance before the end.
[^1067]: Rubric directing that the Triodion (the three-ode canon to the Apostles by St. Joseph the Hymnographer) is chanted without prostrations (metanias), distinguishing it from the Great Canon where three metanias accompany each troparion.
[^1068]: Ode 4 Heirmos of the Triodion to the Apostles, Tone 8: "I have heard, O Lord, the mystery of Thy dispensation" (Услышахъ Господи смотренія твоего таинство / Εἰσακήκοα Κύριε, τὴν ἀκοὴν τῆς σῆς οἰκονομίας), drawing upon the Canticle of Habakkuk (Habakkuk 3:2).
[^1069]: The Twelve Apostles are likened to a mystical twelve-stringed musical instrument (двоенадесятострунный органъ / δωδεκάχорδον ὄργανον), playing the saving melody of the Gospel and confounding evil deceit.
[^1070]: Second canon of the Triodion to the Apostles by St. Theodore the Studite (759–826), Tone 8, sharing the same heirmos.
[^1071]: Ode 4 Heirmos of the Great Canon, Tone 6: "The prophet heard of Thy coming, O Lord" (Услыша пророкъ пришествіе твое Господи / Ἀκήκοεν ὁ προφήτης, τὴν ἔλευσίν σου Κύριε), drawing upon Habakkuk 3:2 and the mystery of the Incarnation from the Virgin.
[^1072]: St. Andrew allegorizes Jacob's ladder (Genesis 28:12) and his two wives (Genesis 29): Leah represents active ascetical practice (πρᾶξις), fruitful in good deeds, while Rachel represents contemplative knowledge and spiritual vision (θεωρία), attained through great labor. "A mind seeing God" invokes the traditional Patristic etymology of Israel (ὁ ὁρῶν Θεόν), leading to the unwaning divine darkness (γνόφος) of mystical theology.
[^1073]: The twelve sons of Jacob (Genesis 35:22–26) are depicted as mystical steps of active ascent, contrasted with Esau (Edom), who sold his birthright for the gratification of sensual desire (Genesis 25:29–34; Hebrews 12:16).
[^1074]: Job on the dungheap (Job 2), his sudden loss of family, wealth, and royal dignity, and his patient endurance; contrasted with the soul's impatience under affliction. Christ's crucifixion offers His Body, Blood, and Spirit for the renewal, cleansing, and restoration of humanity unto the Father.
[^1075]: The Blood and Water issuing from Christ's pierced side (John 19:34) typify the cleansing bath of baptism and the cup of the Eucharist, uniting the Old and New Covenants. St. Andrew weaves together the parables of the wedding garment and foolish virgins (Matthew 22:11–14; 25:1–13) with the Publican and the Pharisee (Luke 18:9–14).
[^1076]: Troparia to St. Mary of Egypt inserted into Ode 4 of the Great Canon, praising her angelic life in the desert after her repentance from the depths of sin.
[^1077]: Ode 5 Heirmos of the Great Canon, Tone 6: "Rising early from the night, O Lover of mankind" (От нощи утренююща, Человѣколюбче / Ἐκ νυκτὸς ὀρθρίζοντα, Φιλάνθρωπε), based upon the Canticle of Isaiah (Isaiah 26:9).
[^1078]: St. Andrew laments falling into the sins of Reuben, who defiled his father's bed (Genesis 35:22; 49:4), and of the brothers who sold Joseph into Egyptian slavery (Genesis 37:28), seeing Joseph as a type of Christ's suffering, burial, and resurrection. Moses placed in the ark of bulrushes by the Nile (Exodus 2:1–10) prefigures preservation from the demonic decree of Pharaoh.
[^1079]: Ode 5 continues the Old Testament typology of the Exodus: the Hebrew midwives Shiphrah and Puah who preserved the male children (Exodus 1:15–21); Moses striking the Egyptian and fleeing to Midian (Exodus 2:11–15); the burning bush on Sinai (Exodus 3:1–6); the rod of Moses parting the Red Sea (Exodus 14:16); Aaron's pure incense offering contrasted with the profane fire of Hophni and Phinehas (1 Kingdoms 2:12–17 [1 Samuel 2:12–17]); and the Egyptian magicians Jannes and Jambres who opposed Moses (Exodus 7:11; 2 Timothy 3:8).
[^1080]: New Testament healing miracles in Ode 5: Christ healing lepers and the paralytic; the woman with the issue of blood healed by touching the hem of His garment (Matthew 9:20–22); the woman bowed down for eighteen years straightened (Luke 13:10–17); and the Samaritan woman at Jacob's well (John 4:5–42).
[^1081]: The washing in the pool of Siloam (John 9:7) as a figure of illumination through tears; Ode 6 Heirmos of the Great Canon, Tone 6: "I cried with my whole heart unto the compassionate God" (Возопихъ всѣмъ сердцемъ моимъ / Ἐβόησα, ἐν ὅλῃ καρδίᾳ μου), based upon the Canticle of Jonah (Jonah 2:2); the judgment upon Dathan and Abiram who were swallowed up by the earth (Numbers 16); and Ephraim stubborn as a wild heifer (Hosea 4:16; Jeremiah 31:18).
[^1082]: Old Testament types in Ode 6: the hand of Moses rendered leprous and restored (Exodus 4:6–7); the Red Sea returning upon Pharaoh's chariots (Exodus 14:26–28); Israel murmuring against the manna and longing for the fleshpots of Egypt (Numbers 11:4–6); the rock struck by Moses pouring forth water (Exodus 17:6; Numbers 20:11; 1 Corinthians 10:4); and Joshua conquering Amalek and defeating the deception of the Gibeonites (Joshua 5–10).
[^1083]: New Testament allusions in Ode 6: the Apostle Peter sinking in the Sea of Galilee and saved by Christ's hand (Matthew 14:28–31); and the Parable of the Lost Coin (Luke 15:8–10), wherein the Word lights the lamp of St. John the Baptist and Forerunner to restore the divine image in humanity.
[^1084]: The solemn Kontakion of the Great Canon, Tone 6: "My soul, my soul, arise, why sleepest thou?" (Душе моя, душе моя, востани, что спиши; / Ψυχή μου ψυχή μου, ἀνάστα, τί καθεύδεις;), a masterpiece of Byzantine hymnography commonly attributed to St. Romanos the Melodist (c. 490–556) or St. Andrew of Crete, sung with its Ikos contemplating the devil wounded and defeated by the medicine-chest of Christ's Incarnation and Passion.
[^1085]: Opening of the prose Synaxarion of the Lenten Triodion appointed for Thursday of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast, commemorating the life, education, monastic tonsure in Jerusalem, and theological and hymnographic output of St. Andrew, Archbishop of Crete (Jerusalemite).
[^1086]: The prose Synaxarion for Thursday of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast concludes its account of the Great Canon. It details the life and ecclesiastical offices of St. Andrew of Crete (Damascene and Jerusalemite), who was sent by Patriarch Theodore of Jerusalem to Constantinople for the Sixth Ecumenical Council (Constantinople III, 680–681), where he defended Orthodox christology against Monothelitism. In Constantinople, St. Andrew was ordained deacon and appointed curator of the patriarchal orphanage (orphanotrophos), later becoming Archbishop of Crete. On his return journey from Constantinople, he fell asleep in the Lord near a harbor called Hieros on the island of Mytilene (Lesbos). The text contrasts the Great Canon (numbering 250 troparia) with ordinary Byzantine canons (having ~30 troparia), and highlights the simultaneous appointment of the Life of St. Mary of Egypt by St. Sophronius, Patriarch of Jerusalem (c. 560–638).
[^1087]: The Beatitudes with Troparia (Блаже́нны съ покло́ны / Μακαρισμοί) appointed at Matins of Thursday of the Fifth Week, chanted in Tone 6 with prostrations (metanias). The hymnographer interleaves the Gospel Beatitudes (Matthew 5:3–12) with penitential strophes drawing upon the repentance of the Good Thief (Luke 23:42) and Old Testament types of Judges and early rulers. Manoah and his barren wife (Judges 13) prefigure divine promise, while Samson shorn of his hair by Delilah (Judges 16) allegorizes the soul stripped of virtuous deeds by carnal passion.
[^1088]: Old Testament types in the Beatitudes troparia: Samson conquered by Delilah (Judges 16); Barak and Deborah (Judges 4–5); Jephthah the judge (Judges 11); Jael slaying Sisera with a tent peg (Judges 4:21), a classic patristic type of the Cross defeating Satan; and Jephthah offering his daughter as a vowed sacrifice (Judges 11:30–40), allegorized as the soul immolating carnal passions before God.
[^1089]: Gideon's fleece receiving heavenly dew and his soldiers lapping water like dogs at the spring of Harod (Judges 6:36–40; 7:4–7); the condemnation of the high priest Eli for failing to discipline his sacrilegious sons Hophni and Phinehas (1 Kingdoms 2:12–36 [1 Samuel 2:12–36]); the Levite of Ephraim cutting the body of his abused concubine into twelve pieces and sending them throughout the tribes of Israel to expose the sin of the Benjaminites of Gibeah (Judges 19); and the barren Hannah praying silently in the tabernacle at Shiloh, moving only her lips, to give birth to Samuel (1 Kingdoms 1 [1 Samuel 1]).
[^1090]: Samuel raised in the tabernacle at Shiloh and dedicated to the Lord (1 Kingdoms 1–3); King David anointed with the horn of divine oil by Samuel (1 Kingdoms 16:13), urging the soul to receive the mystical anointing of penitential tears; followed by the Beatitudes Doxasticon and Theotokion confessing Christ as one divine Person (Hypostasis) in two distinct unconfused natures, echoing the Definition of Chalcedon (451).
[^1091]: Ode 7 Heirmos of the Great Canon, Tone 6: "We have sinned, we have transgressed, we have done unrighteously before Thee" (Согрѣши́хомъ, беззако́нновахомъ / Ἡμάρтомен, ἠνομήσαμεν, ἠδικήσαμεν ἐνώπιόν σου), based upon the Canticle of the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace (Daniel 3:26–45 / Prayer of Azariah).
[^1092]: Saul seeking the lost asses of his father Kish and unexpectedly receiving the royal anointing from Samuel (1 Kingdoms 9–10 [1 Samuel 9–10]), warning the soul not to prefer irrational passions over the Kingdom of Christ.
[^1093]: King David's double sin of adultery with Bathsheba and the murder of Uriah the Hittite (2 Kingdoms 11 [2 Samuel 11]), contrasted with his immediate confession before the prophet Nathan and his penitential composition of Psalm 50 (LXX); Uzzah (За́нъ) struck dead for irreverently touching the Ark of the Covenant when the oxen stumbled (2 Kingdoms 6:6–7); Absalom's unnatural rebellion against his father David and the defilement of his father's concubines (2 Kingdoms 15–16); and the wicked counsel of Ahithophel overthrown by the providence of God (2 Kingdoms 15–17).
[^1094]: Solomon's exemplary wisdom corrupted in old age through his foreign wives who led his heart after strange gods (3 Kingdoms 11 [1 Kings 11]); Rehoboam provoking the division of the kingdom by despising the counsel of the elders (3 Kingdoms 12); Jeroboam leading the northern tribes into golden-calf idolatry at Bethel and Dan (3 Kingdoms 12:26–33); and Ahab's tyrannical wickedness and idolatry incited by Queen Jezebel (3 Kingdoms 16:30–33; 21).
[^1095]: Elijah calling down fire from heaven upon the two captains of fifty sent by King Ahaziah (4 Kingdoms 1 [2 Kings 1]) and slaughtering the 450 prophets of Baal at Mount Carmel (3 Kingdoms 18:40); the heaven shut from rain for three and a half years during Ahab's reign (3 Kingdoms 17:1; Luke 4:25), and the destitute widow of Zarephath (Sarepta) sustaining the prophet with her last handful of meal and cruse of oil (3 Kingdoms 17:8–24); and King Manasseh's horrific idolatry followed by heartfelt repentance in Babylonian captivity (2 Chronicles 33:11–13; Prayer of Manasseh).
[^1096]: David's penitential plea in Psalm 50:14 (LXX): "Restore unto me the joy of Thy salvation" (Возда́ждь мѝ ра́дость спасе́нїя твоегѡ̀ / Ἀπόδος μοι τὴν ἀγαλλίασιν τοῦ σωτηρίου σου); and King Hezekiah weeping upon his bed when stricken with mortal sickness, granted fifteen additional years of life through the prophet Isaiah (4 Kingdoms 20:1–6; Isaiah 38:1–5).
[^1097]: Ode 8 Heirmos of the Triodion to the Holy Apostles, Tone 8, by St. Joseph the Hymnographer: "The beginningless King of glory" (Безнача́льнаго Царя̀ сла́вы / Τὸν ἐν ὄρει ἁγίῳ δοξασθέντα), drawing upon the Canticle of the Three Holy Children (Daniel 3:57–88). The Apostles are likened to glowing coals of immaterial fire and trumpets of the Word casting down the spiritual walls of Jericho (Joshua 6).
[^1098]: Second Triodion of Ode 8 by St. Theodore the Studite (759–826), sharing the same heirmos, praising the Holy Apostles as divine architects who established the Church upon the foundation of Christ and overthrew the deception of pagan idolatry.
[^1099]: Ode 8 Heirmos of the Great Canon, Tone 6: "Him Whom the heavenly hosts glorify" (Е҆го́же во́инства небе́сная сла́вятъ / Ὃν στρατιαὶ οὐρανῶν δοξάζουσι), based upon the Song of the Three Holy Children (Daniel 3:57–88).
[^1100]: Old Testament types of ascetic ascent and judgment in Ode 8: Elijah taken up to heaven in a chariot of fire (4 Kingdoms 2:11); Elisha parting the Jordan with Elijah's mantle and receiving a double portion of his spirit (4 Kingdoms 2:9–14); the hospitable Shunammite woman receiving Elisha (4 Kingdoms 4:8–37); Gehazi struck with Naaman's leprosy for his covetous deceit (4 Kingdoms 5:20–27); King Uzziah stricken with leprosy for presumptuously burning incense upon the altar (2 Chronicles 26:16–21); and the Ninevites repenting at the preaching of Jonah (Jonah 3:5–10).
[^1101]: Jeremiah cast into the muddy cistern of Malchiah during the siege of Jerusalem and lamenting over the ruins of Zion (Jeremiah 38:6; Lamentations); Jonah fleeing to Tarshish because he foreknew God's boundless mercy would spare Nineveh (Jonah 1:3; 4:2); Daniel unharmed in the lions' den (Daniel 6); and the Three Holy Children unharmed in the fiery furnace (Daniel 3).
[^1102]: New Testament archetypes of repentance in Ode 8: the Good Thief (Luke 23:42); Peter weeping bitterly after his threefold denial (Matthew 26:75); the Publican in the temple (Luke 18:13); the sinful woman washing Christ's feet with tears (Luke 7:37–38); the Canaanite woman pleading for her demon-possessed daughter (Matthew 15:22–28); the woman with the issue of blood healed by touching Christ's hem (Matthew 9:20–22); Martha and Mary weeping at the tomb of Lazarus (John 11); and the Good Shepherd rescuing the lost sheep (Luke 15:4–7).
[^1103]: The hymnographer evokes the Dread Judgment and the fiery river (Daniel 7:9–10), praising the ascetic flight of St. Mary of Egypt, who astonished the desert elder Zosimas by appearing as an earthly angel, and imploring the intercession of St. Andrew of Crete as an initiate of repentance.
[^1104]: Ode 9 Heirmos of the Triodion to the Holy Apostles, Tone 8, by St. Joseph the Hymnographer: "Thee we confess to be truly the Mother of God" (Вои́стиннꙋ Богоро́дицꙋ тя̀ и҆сповѣ́дꙋемъ / Ἀληθῶς Θεοτόκον, σὲ ὁμολογοῦμεν), addressing the Apostles as spiritual springs of water and salt of the earth (Matthew 5:13) cleansing the soul from corrupt passions.
[^1105]: Second Triodion of Ode 9 by St. Theodore the Studite, with its Heirmos: "Thee, mediatress of heaven and earth" (Тя̀ небесѐ и҆ землѝ хода́таицꙋ / Τὴν οὐρανοῦ καὶ γῆς μεσίτην), honoring the Apostles as divine fishermen catching rational fish with the net of the Gospel (Matthew 4:19; Luke 5:10).
[^1106]: Ode 9 Heirmos of the Great Canon, Tone 6: "The childbearing of a seedless conception is ineffable" (Безсѣ́меннагѡ Зача́тїа Рождество̀ несказа́нное / Ἀσπόρου συλλήψεως, ὁ τόκος ἀνερμήνευτος), drawing upon the Canticle of the Theotokos (Magnificat, Luke 1:46–55) and the mystery of the virginal conception.
[^1107]: The opening of Ode 9 of the Great Canon presents a profound psychological and spiritual diagnosis of total human brokenness: the mind covered in sores (mind wounded), the body sickened, the spirit languishing, discourse failing, life deadened, and the end at the doors. St. Andrew then opens his final scriptural recapitulation with Moses' book of Genesis (Міробытїѐ / Κοσμογένεσις), calling the soul to examine the righteous and the sinners from the dawn of creation.
[^1108]: Ode 9 troparia of the Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete, Tone 6, continuing the New Testament scriptural witness. St. Andrew passes from Old Testament archetypes to the Gospel dispensations: Christ incarnate calling tax collectors and harlots (Matthew 9:10–13), the worship of the Magi (Matthew 2:1–12), the Massacre of the Innocents as the first martyr-witnesses (Matthew 2:16–18), the righteous Simeon and Anna (Luke 2:25–38), the Lord's forty-day fast and threefold wilderness temptation (Matthew 4:1–11), the preaching and martyrdom of St. John the Baptist under Herod Antipas and Herodias (Matthew 14:1–12), and the marriage feast in Cana of Galilee (John 2:1–11).
[^1109]: Continuation of Ode 9 of the Great Canon, celebrating Christ's healing ministry and the evangelical figures of penitence: the paralytic carrying his bed (Mark 2:1–12; John 5:1–15); the raising of the son of the widow of Nain (Luke 7:11–17); the healing of the centurion's servant (Matthew 8:5–13); the Samaritan woman at the well of Sychar (John 4:5–42); the woman with the issue of blood (Matthew 9:20–22); the healing of lepers, blind, and lame (Luke 7:22); the raising of Jairus' daughter (Mark 5:21–43); the justification of the Publican over the proud Pharisee (Luke 18:9–14); the conversion of Zacchaeus (Luke 19:1–10); the sinful woman anointing Christ's feet in the house of Simon the Pharisee (Luke 7:36–50); the prophetic woes against Chorazin, Bethsaida, and Capernaum (Matthew 11:20–24); the persistent faith of the Syro-Phoenician/Canaanite woman (Matthew 15:21–28); and the confession of the Good Thief on the Cross (Luke 23:39–43).
[^1110]: Conclusion of the Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete, Ode 9. St. Andrew invokes the cosmic sympathy of creation at Christ's Crucifixion (Matthew 27:45–54), offers spiritual brokenness of heart (LXX Ps. 50:19) in place of worthy fruits, and concludes with fervent supplications to St. Mary of Egypt (honoring her miraculous, immaterial crossing of the Jordan River) and to St. Andrew of Crete himself as pastor and guide of repentance, sealed by a Trinitarian triadicon and the imperial city protection theotokion. At the Aposticha, the penitential automelon in Tone 8 ("Having fallen into robber-infested paths" / Въ разбо́йническїѧ пꙋти̑ впа́дши) develops the parable of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10:30–37).
[^1111]: Prokeimenon of the Sixth Hour, Psalm 96 (LXX 96:1), Tone 6: "The Lord is King, let the earth rejoice; let the many islands be glad" (Ὁ Κύριος ἐβασίλευσεν, ἀγαλλιάσθω ἡ γῆ / Гдⷭ҇ь воцр҃и́сѧ, да ра́дꙋетсѧ землѧ̀).
[^1112]: Sixth Hour Paremia: Isaiah 42:5–16, the First Servant Song of Isaiah, prophesying the Messiah as the Covenant of the people and Light to the Gentiles, opening the eyes of the blind and delivering prisoners from the dungeon.
[^1113]: Prokeimenon following the Isaiah reading, Psalm 97 (LXX 97:1–2), Tone 6: "Sing unto the Lord a new song, for marvelous things has the Lord done" (Ἄισατε τῷ Κυρίῳ ᾆσμα καινόν / Воспо́йте Гдⷭ҇ви пѣ́снь но́вꙋ, ꙗ҆́кѡ ди̑вна сотворѝ Гдⷭ҇ь).
[^1114]: Thursday of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast, at Vespers. Following the extensive vigil of the Great Canon (the Matins of Thursday celebrated on Wednesday evening), this service marks the return to the standard Lenten weekday cycle, leading into the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts.
[^1115]: Codicological and typikon rubric on the Kathisma and sounding of the semantron (klepalo): On account of the immense physical labor of the preceding nocturnal vigil of the Great Canon, Kathisma 12 (Psalms 85–90) replaces the standard 18th Kathisma ("Unto the Lord when I was afflicted" / Пѣ́сни степе́ней), and the lamplighting semantron is struck softly (поко́рꙋ, lit. with subdued or gentle cadence) to spare the exhausted brethren.
[^1116]: Melodic model Podoben in Tone 1: "O all-praised martyrs" (Всехва́льнїи мꙋ́ченицы / Πανεύφημοι μάρτυρες).
[^1117]: Melodic model Podoben in Tone 6: "Archangelically let us hymn" (А҆рха́гг҃льски воспои́мъ / Ἀρχαγγελικῶς ἀνυμνήσωμεν).
[^1118]: Vespers Prokeimenon, Psalm 98 (LXX 98:5, 1), Tone 7: "Exalt the Lord our God, and worship the footstool of His feet, for He is holy" (Ὑψοῦτε Κύριον τὸν Θεὸν ἡμῶν / Возноси́те Гдⷭ҇а Бг҃а на́шего).
[^1119]: Vespers First Paremia: Genesis 18:20–33, recounting Abraham's intercession before the Lord for the righteous of Sodom and Gomorrah, bargaining from fifty righteous down to ten.
[^1120]: Historical monastic fasting rubric (трапеза): Documenting the divergence between the Studite monastic rule (which mandates xerophagy / dry fasting throughout the fifth week on account of its penitential character) and the Athonite Typikon of the Holy Mountain (which permits two cooked dishes seasoned with oil and two cups of wine on the Great Canon vigil, on the feast of the Forty Martyrs of Sebaste, on the Finding of the Head of the Forerunner, on Wednesday of the Third Week of Lent / Mid-Lent, and on the Akathist Saturday), leaving the choice to individual ascetic conscience before God.
[^1121]: Markian rubric (Зрѝ / Nota bene): Typikon resolution governing the rare calendar concurrence of the Feast of the Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos (March 25) falling on Thursday of the Fifth Week of Lent. In such an occurrence, the celebration of the Great Canon of St. Andrew of Crete is transferred earlier in the week, sung at Matins on Tuesday (served on Monday evening), in order to maintain the uncompromised festal integrity of the Annunciation.
[^1122]: Friday of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast, at Matins. This day serves as the liturgical preparation and threshold for the great celebration of Saturday of the Akathist (the Praise of the Most Holy Theotokos).
[^1123]: Melodic model Podoben in Tone 1: "While the soldiers were guarding Thy tomb" (Гро́бъ тво́й Сп҃се / Τὸν τάφον σου Σωτήρ).
[^1124]: Melodic model Podoben in Tone 6: "O hope of the world" (Оу҆пова́нїе мі́ра / Ἡ ἐλπὶς τοῦ κόσμου).
[^1125]: Triodion (Odes 5, 8, 9) by St. Joseph the Hymnographer, Tone 1. Ode 5 Heirmos: "Thou Who hast enlightened with the radiance of Thy coming" (Просвѣти́вый сїѧ́нїемъ прише́ствїѧ твоегѡ̀ / Ὁ φωτίσας τῇ ἐλλάμψει), focusing on the veneration of the Cross, Christ stretching out His hands upon the tree as He stretched out the heavens like a curtain (Psalm 103:2).
[^1126]: Second Triodion by St. Theodore the Studite, Tone 6. Ode 5 Heirmos: "From the night rising early, O Lover of mankind" (Ѿ но́щи ѹ҆́тренююща Человѣколю́бче / Ἐκ νυκτὸς ὀρθρίζοντα Φιλάνθρωπε), addressing Christ's voluntary Crucifixion, the piercing of His hands and side by the Judeans, and the restoration of Adam through the tree of the Cross.
[^1127]: Ode 8 Heirmos of the Triodion of St. Joseph, Tone 1: "The marvel beyond nature" (Чꙋ́да преесте́ственнагѡ / Θαύματος ὑπερφυοῦς), praising Christ for blunting the stings of death and the devil, and gathering all mankind through His outstretched palms on the Cross.
[^1128]: Ode 8 Heirmos of the Second Triodion of St. Theodore the Studite, Tone 6: "The song of the youths" (Ѻ҆трокѡ́въ пѣ́снь / Παίδων εὐαγῶν), evoking the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace (Daniel 3) blessing the crucified and life-giving Lord.
[^1129]: Ode 9 Heirmos of the Triodion of St. Joseph, Tone 1: "The type of the pure childbearing" (Ѻ҆́бразъ чи́стагѡ рождества̀ / Τύπον τῆς ἁγνῆς λοχείας), meditating on the Prophet Elijah beholding God in the gentle breeze on Mount Horeb (3 Kings / 1 Kings 19:11–12) after fasting and prayer, and Moses lifting up the bronze serpent on the tree (Numbers 21:8–9; John 3:14).
[^1130]: Ode 9 Heirmos of the Second Triodion of St. Theodore the Studite, Tone 6: "The childbearing of a seedless conception is ineffable" (Безсѣ́меннагѡ Зача́тїа Рождество̀ несказа́нное / Ἀσπόρου συλλήψεως, ὁ τόκος ἀνερμήνευτος), marveling at the infinite condescension of the Master accepting a reproachful death upon the Cross for the sake of His servant.
[^1131]: Friday of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast, conclusion of Ode 9 of the Triodia (St. Joseph and St. Theodore the Studite). The Trinitarian triadicon ("O Father of the Only-begotten Son, Thou Sole Begetter of the One...") and the commemoration of the physical sufferings of Christ's members (head, cheeks, hands, side, Cross). At the Aposticha, the penitential Idiomelon in Tone 8 ("O Christ God, Who wast nailed to the Cross with robbers...") applies the parable of the Good Samaritan (Luke 10:30–37) to the soul wounded by spiritual robbers and healed by the oil and wine flowing from the Savior's pierced side.
[^1132]: Friday of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast, Sixth Hour. Troparion of Prophecy in Tone 8 ("O Thou Who art sung by all, O Christ our God, Who didst shake the earth..."), Prokeimenon (Psalm 100), and Paremia from Isaiah 45:11–17. The prophetic text exalts the Lord as the Creator of earth, the heavens, and the host of stars, announcing Cyrus as the righteous king who shall rebuild Jerusalem and set the captives free without price, and foretelling the submission of Egypt, Ethiopia, and Saba to the God of Israel. Prokeimenon (Psalm 101).
[^1133]: Friday Evening of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast, at Vespers (the liturgical vigil of Saturday of the Akathist). At "Lord, I have cried," the Idiomelon in Tone 8 ("As I went forth from the Jerusalem of Thy divine commandments...") resumes the Good Samaritan motif, followed by three Prosomoia of the Most Holy Theotokos doubled to six in Tone 6, set to the melodic model Podoben "Having laid aside all" (Ὅλην ἀποθέμενοι / Всѐ ѿло́жше). These stichera open the dialogue of the Annunciation (Luke 1:26–38) between the Archangel Gabriel and the Virgin Mary, setting forth the typology of the untilled earth, the burning bush (Exodus 3:2), Jacob's ladder (Genesis 28:12), and the jar of manna (Exodus 16:33).
[^1134]: Vespers Doxasticon in Tone 2 ("The mystery hidden from the ages is revealed today..."), celebrating the exchange of divine and human natures: God becomes man that He may deify Adam. First Paremia: Genesis 22:1–17, recounting the Akedah (the binding and offering of Isaac on Mount Moriah), the preeminent Old Testament type of the voluntary sacrifice of the Only-begotten Son upon the Cross.
[^1135]: Conclusion of the First Paremia, Genesis 22:17–18, confirming the divine covenant and blessing upon Abraham's seed as the stars of heaven and sand of the seashore. Second Paremia: Proverbs 17:17–18:5, offering ethical instructions on brotherhood in distress, the avoidance of frivolous surety, the joy of a wise son, the medicinal virtue of a cheerful heart, and the heinousness of perverting justice. Historical monastic fasting rubric permitting wine at the refectory to sustain the brethren for the arduous nocturnal vigil of the Akathist, with Small Compline and Midnight Office read in cells, and the Canon for the Departed chanted in the crypt.
[^1136]: Physical leaf p342 = Printed Folio 338 + 4. Cross-reference to Thursday of the Great Canon (Fifth Week of the Great Fast), detailing the opening order of Matins with the Royal Office, Six Psalms, and "God is the Lord" in Tone 8.
[^1137]: Saturday of the Fifth Week of the Great Fast, the Akathist of the Most Holy Theotokos (Saturday of the Akathist / Praise of the Most Holy Theotokos). The festal troparion in Tone 8, "Having received the secret command in spirit" (Τὸ προσταχθὲν μυστικῶς / Повелѣ́нное та́йнѡ), introduces the celebration. Following the 16th Kathisma (Psalms 109–117), the celebrated automelon Kontakion in Tone 8, "To thee, the Champion Leader" (Τῇ ὑπερμάχῳ στρατηγῷ / Взбра́нной воево́дѣ), is chanted slowly and melodiously in the midst of the nave. The rubric prescribes the censing of the entire church and the liturgical division of the 24 stanzas of the Akathist into four stases of six stanzas each, chanted by the priest standing in the holy sanctuary before the icon of the Mother of God.
[^1138]: First Stasis of the Akathist Hymn (Stanzas 1–6, Greek alphabet Alpha through Zeta, A–Z): Ikos 1 (A: Ἄγγελος πρωτοστάτης / А҆́ггелъ предста́тель) with its twelve angelic salutations ("Rejoice..."); Kontakion 2 (B: Βλέπουσα ἡ Ἁγία / Ви́дѧщи Ст҃а́ѧ); Ikos 2 (G: Γνῶσιν ἄγνωστον / Ра́зꙋмъ недоразꙋмѣва́емый); Kontakion 3 (D: Δύναμις τοῦ Ὑψίστου / Си́ла Вы́шнѧгѡ); Ikos 3 (E: Ἔχουσα θεοδόχον / И҆мꙋ́щи бг҃опрїя́тнꙋю); and Kontakion 4 (Z: Ζάλην ἔνδοθεν ἔχων / Бꙋ́рю внꙋ́трь и҆мѣ́ѧ), portraying the Annunciation, the Visitation of the Virgin to Elizabeth (Luke 1:39–56), and the perplexity and reassurance of the righteous Joseph (Matthew 1:18–25).
[^1139]: Second Stasis of the Akathist Hymn (Stanzas 7–12, Greek alphabet Eta through Lambda, H–L): Ikos 4 (H: Ἤκουσαν οἱ ποιμένες / Слы́шаша па́стырїе); Kontakion 5 (Th: Θεοδρόμον ἀστέρα / Бг҃оте́чнꙋю ѕвѣздꙋ̀); Ikos 5 (I: Ἴδον παῖδες Χалдаίων / Ви́дѣша ѻ҆́троцы халде́йстїи); Kontakion 6 (K: Κήρυκες θεοφόροι / Проповѣ́дницы бг҃оно́снїи); and Ikos 6 (L: Λάмψας ἐν τῇ Αἰγύπτῳ / Возсїя́вый во Е҆гѵ́птѣ), recounting the adoration of the shepherds at Bethlehem (Luke 2:8–20), the journey and adoration of the Magi from Babylon (Matthew 2:1–12), and the Flight into Egypt with the casting down of idols (Isaiah 19:1). Interspersed with the reading of the 17th Kathisma (The Blameless / LXX Ps. 118) and the patristic reading from the Akathist Synaxarion.
[^1140]: Conclusion of the Second Stasis with Kontakion 7 (M: Μέλλοντος Συμεῶνος / Хотѧ́щꙋ Сѷмеѡ́нꙋ), celebrating the Presentation of the Lord in the Temple (Luke 2:25–35). The Canon of the Akathist in Tone 4, composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (ca. 816–886), bearing the Greek acrostic: "Receptacle of joy, to thee alone it is fitting to rejoice. Joseph" (Χαρᾶς δοχεῖον, σοὶ πρέπει χαίρειν μόνῃ. Ἰωσήφ / Ра́дости прїя́телище, тебѣ̀ подоба́етъ ра́доватися є҆ди́нѣй), with Heirmoi of the 4th Tone ("I shall open my mouth" and "Establish thy singers, O Theotokos"). The stanzas extol the Mother of God as the living Book of Christ, the untilled field bearing the Bread of life, and the spotless heifer. Opening of the Third Stasis with Ikos 7 (N: Νέαν ἔδειξε κτίσιν / Но́вꙋю показа̀ тва́рь).
[^1141]: Third Stasis of the Akathist Hymn (Stanzas 13–18, Greek alphabet Nu through Sigma, N–S): completion of Ikos 7; Kontakion 8 (X: Ξένον τόκον ἰδόντες / Стра́нное Ржⷭ҇тво̀); Ikos 8 (O: Ὅλος ἦν ἐν τοῖς κάτω / Ве́сь бѣ̀ въ ни́жнихъ); Kontakion 9 (P: Πᾶσα φύσις ἀγγέλων / Вся́кое є҆стество̀ А҆́гг҃льское); Ikos 9 (R: Ῥήτορας πολυφθόγγους / Вѣті̑ѧ многовѣща̑нныѧ); and Kontakion 10 (S: Σῶσαι θέλων τὸν κόσμον / Спастѝ хотя̀ мі́ръ), setting forth the theological contemplation of the Incarnation, the paradox of the virginal childbearing confounding pagan philosophers and Athenian sophists, and the good Shepherd calling all creation unto Himself.
[^1142]: Sessional Hymn after the Third Stasis of the Akathist Hymn, Tone 1, set to the melodic model Podoben "While the soldiers were guarding Thy tomb" (Τὸν τάφον σου Σωτήρ / Гро́бъ тво́й), celebrating the Archangel Gabriel's Annunciation to the Virgin Mary at Nazareth. Ode 4 Heirmos ("He Who sits in glory upon the throne of the Godhead...") and Canon of the Akathist in Tone 4 composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (ca. 816–886), featuring the Greek alphabetical acrostic letters Σ-Ο-Ι  Π-Ρ-Ε-Π-Ε-Ι (σοὶ πρέπει).
[^1143]: Ode 5 of the Canon of the Akathist in Tone 4, Heirmos "All things were amazed at thy divine glory," with stanzas carrying the Greek acrostic letters Ο-Ι-Π-Ρ-Ε (continuing the acrostic phrase σοὶ πρέπει and initiating χαίρειν), extolling the Theotokos as the Way of life, the spiritual Paradise bearing the Tree of Life, and the unquarried mountain.
[^1144]: Liturgical rubric governing the insertion of the canon of the local monastery or temple patron saint between Odes 6 and 7. Ode 6 of the Akathist Canon in Tone 4, with stanzas carrying the Greek acrostic letters Π-Ε-Ι-Χ (completing πρέπει and opening χαίρειν). Followed by the Four-Ode Canon (Tetraodion) of St. Joseph the Hymnographer in Tone 6 ("The sea of life surging with tempest"), bearing the acrostic "This song is by Joseph" (Сїѧ̀ пѣ́снь І҆ѡ́сифова), commemorating the holy martyrs and the faithful departed.
[^1145]: Second Canon of Ode 6 in Tone 5, Heirmos "Out of the whale, the Prophet" (Ѿ ки́та проро́ка), offering supplication for the holy martyrs who vanquished tormentors, swords, and fire, concluding with a Trinitarian doxology and Theotokion.
[^1146]: Commemoration of the holy martyrs and supplication for the faithful departed following Ode 6. Scriptural verses: Psalm 67:36 ("Marvelous is God in His saints, the God of Israel") and Psalm 24:13 ("Their souls shall abide in prosperity"), accompanied by the Katavasia of Tone 5 ("Thou didst deliver the Prophet from the sea-monster...").
[^1147]: Fourth and concluding Stasis of the Akathist Hymn (Stanzas 19–24, Greek alphabet Tau through Omega, T–Ω): Ikos 10 (T: Τεῖχος εἶ τῶν παρθένων / Стѣна̀ є҆сѝ дѣ́вамъ) with its twelve angelic salutations invoking the Theotokos as the pillar of virginity, gate of salvation, and guide of divine purity; Kontakion 11 (Y: Ὕμνος ἅπας ἡττᾶται / Пѣ́нїе всѧ́кое побѣжда́етсѧ); Ikos 11 (Ph: Φωτοδόχον λαμπάδα / Свѣтопрїе́мнꙋю свѣщꙋ̀); and Kontakion 12 (Ch: Χάριν δοῦναι θελήσας / Бл҃года́ть да́ти восхотѣ́въ).
[^1148]: Conclusion of the Fourth Stasis of the Akathist Hymn: Ikos 12 (Ps: Ψάλλοντές σου τὸν τόκον / Пою́ще твоѐ ржⷭ҇тво̀), extolling the Mother of God as the living temple, the Holy of Holies, the ark gilded by the Spirit, the unshakeable tower of the Church, and the invincible wall of the realm; and Kontakion 13 (O: Ὦ πανύμνητε Μῆτερ / Ѽ всепѣ́таѧ Мт҃и), chanted thrice, followed by the repetition of Ikos 1 ("An Archangel was sent...") and Kontakion 1 ("To thee, the Champion Leader").
[^1149]: Synaxarion of the Saturday of the Akathist (Praise of the Most Holy Theotokos), commemorating the miraculous divine deliverance of Constantinople from the joint siege of the Persians (under King Khosrow II and his general Shahrbaraz / Sarbaros) and the Avars/Scythians (under the Chagan / Khagan) in 626 AD during the reign of Emperor Heraclius (610–641). While the emperor was campaigning in Persia, Patriarch Sergius I led liturgical processions along the city walls bearing the Icon of Christ Not-Made-by-Hands, the relic of the True Cross, and the Robe (Maphorion) of the Virgin Mary, and Patrician Bonus directed the military defense. A miraculous storm destroyed the enemy fleet at Blachernae in the Golden Horn, prompting the entire populace to maintain an all-night thanksgiving vigil standing upright, giving origin to the title "Akathist" (unseated hymn).
[^1150]: Continuation of the Akathist Synaxarion, documenting subsequent historical deliverances of the Imperial City attributed to the Mother of God: the first Arab siege under Emperor Constantine IV Pogonatus (674–678 AD) with the destruction of the Saracen fleet at Syllaeum, and the second Arab siege under Emperor Leo III the Isaurian (717–718 AD) through the protection of the Holy Cross and the Hodigitria icon, resulting in the destruction of the Muslim fleet by storms and famine. The rubric notes that whereas during other readings the congregation sits, during this thanksgiving office all stand upright.
[^1151]: Ode 7 of the Canon of the Akathist in Tone 4, Heirmos "The godly-minded youths worshipped not creation" (Не послꙋжи́ша тва́ри), with stanzas carrying the Greek acrostic letters Α-Ι-Ρ-Ε-Ι (completing the word χαίρειν, with the final letter Nu following in Ode 8), praising the Virgin Mary as the spiritual chariot of the Sun, the mystical rod blooming with the unfading flower, and the living scroll inscribed by the finger of God the Father.
[^1152]: Second Canon of Ode 7 in Tone 5, Heirmos "Blessed art Thou, O God" (Бл҃гослове́нъ є҆сѝ Бж҃е), with martyrial troparia, Trinitarian doxology, and Theotokion. Opening of Ode 8 of the Canon of the Akathist, Heirmos "The pious youths in the furnace," carrying the Greek acrostic letters Ν (completing χαίρειν), Μ, and Ο (initiating the word μόνῃ).
[^1153]: Conclusion of Ode 8 of the Canon of the Akathist in Tone 4, with stanzas carrying the Greek acrostic letters Ν and Η (completing the word μόνῃ / Μ-Ο-Ν-Η), celebrating the Theotokos as the restoration of the dead, the cleansing of lepers, and the healing of diseases. Followed by the second canon in Tone 6 ("Out of the flame for the venerable...") and third canon in Tone 5 ("The Creator of creation, Whom the Angels dread..."), honoring the holy martyrs and praying for the departed.
[^1154]: Ode 9 of the Canon of the Akathist in Tone 4, Heirmos "Every earthborn mortal" (Всѧ́къ земноро́дный), carrying the final five letters of the Greek acrostic forming the name of the hymnographer: Ι-Ω-Σ-Η-Φ (Ἰωσήφ / Joseph), praising the Theotokos as the unwaning star, the open Eden, the vessel of inexhaustible Myrrh, the pillar of fire, and the immaculate dove. Followed by the second canon in Tone 6 ("God it is impossible for men to see") and third canon in Tone 5 ("Rejoice, O Isaiah").
[^1155]: Exapostilarion of the Saturday of the Akathist, set to the melodic model Podoben "Give ear, ye women" (Γυναῖκες ἀκουτίσθητε / Жены̀ ᲂу҆слы́шите): "The mystery hidden from the ages is made known today..." At the Praises (Lauds), three Prosomoia of the Annunciation doubled to four in Tone 4, set to the melodic model Podoben "As one valiant among martyrs" (Ὡς γενναῖον ἐν μάρτυσιν / Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ), followed by the festal Doxasticon in Tone 4 ("The Mother of God heard a tongue which she knew not..."), the Great Doxology, and Dismissal.
[^1156]: Divine Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom on the Saturday of the Akathist. At the Beatitudes, troparia from Odes 3 and 6 of the Akathist Canon are appointed. Scriptural pericopes: Prokeimenon in Tone 3, the Magnificat (Luke 1:46–48); Epistle of the day, Hebrews 9:24–28 (Pericope 322); Epistle of the Theotokos, Hebrews 9:1–7 (Pericope 320); Alleluia in Tone 8, Psalm 131:8, 1; Gospel of the day, Mark 8:34–9:1 (Pericope 35); Gospel of the Theotokos, Luke 10:38–42, 11:27–28 (Pericope 54); Communion Hymn, Psalm 115:4 ("I will take the cup of salvation..."). Refectory rule permitting two cooked courses with oil and wine.
[^1157]: Physical leaf p317 = Printed Folio 313 + 4. Iconographic cross-reference: the full-page woodcut plate of the Crucifixion of our Lord Jesus Christ on Golgotha reappears identically at the conclusion of Saturday of the Akathist on folio 406 (physical leaf p410), serving as a visual monument preceding the services of the Fifth Sunday of the Great Fast (Commemoration of our Venerable Mother Mary of Egypt).
[^1158]: Commemoration of our Venerable Mother Mary of Egypt on the Fifth Sunday of the Great Fast. The Slavonic rubric notes the historical layering of the Byzantine rite: the ancient Sabaite and Constantinopolitan Typika appointed only the Resurrectional services of the Octoechos with the Menaion of the calendar date, while the later Studite and neo-Sabaitic synthesis (from the 11th–12th centuries onwards) definitively transferred the commemoration of St. Mary of Egypt (historically celebrated on April 1) to the Fifth Sunday of Lent, alongside the Great Canon sung on the preceding Thursday.
[^1159]: Slavonic: Ѿ чꙋдесѐ но́вагѡ (Greek: Ὢ καινοῦ θαύματος). Sessional/Theotokion of Tone 2, Dogmatikon of the Octoechos.
[^1160]: Liturgical structure of Matins for the Fifth Sunday of Lent: the three canons appointed are (1) the Resurrectional Canon from the Octoechos of the current Tone (with its Theotokion stanzas) counted for 6 troparia; (2) the Triodion Canon on the Rich Man and Lazarus in Tone 8 (composed by St. Theodore the Studite) for 4 troparia; and (3) the Triodion Canon for St. Mary of Egypt in Tone 6 (also attributed to the Studite tradition) for 4 troparia, totaling 14 troparia per ode.
[^1161]: The Lenten Sunday themes in the Studite Triodion are organized typologically: the Fifth Sunday pairs the Gospel parable of the Rich Man and Poor Lazarus (Luke 16:19–31) with the life of St. Mary of Egypt as the archetypal model of radical repentance and ascetic transformation.
[^1162]: Order of the Sunday Matins conclusion: the Great Doxology is sung, followed by the Dismissal Troparion of the Resurrection, the Great Dismissal, the Doxasticon of the Resurrectional Matins Gospel (the Evangelical Sticheron), and the First Hour.
[^1163]: Divine Liturgy of St. Basil the Great on the Fifth Sunday of Lent: the Beatitudes from the Octoechos for 8 troparia. Prokeimenon of the Resurrectional Tone, and for St. Mary of Egypt in Tone 4, Psalm 67:36 ("God is wonderful in His saints").
[^1164]: Scriptural readings for the Fifth Sunday of Lent: Epistle of the Sunday, Hebrews 9:11–14 (Pericope 321 from the midpoint); Epistle of the Venerable Woman, Galatians 3:23–29 (Pericope 208); Gospel of the Sunday, Mark 10:32–45 (Pericope 37, Christ's third prophecy of His Passion and the request of the sons of Zebedee); Gospel of the Venerable Woman, Luke 7:36–50 (Pericope 33, the woman who was a sinner anointing the feet of Jesus in the house of Simon the Pharisee).
[^1165]: Refectory rule for Sunday of Lent: two cooked meals with wine and oil are permitted, with abstention from meat, dairy, eggs, and fish.
[^1166]: The opening sticheron of Sunday evening marks the liturgical transition to the Sixth Week of the Great Fast (Palm Week), introducing the prefestal themes of the Entrance of our Lord into Jerusalem and the raising of Lazarus.
[^1167]: Physical leaf p179 = Printed Folio 175 + 4. Cross-reference to the Great Prokeimenon of Sunday evening in Tone 8: "Thou hast given an inheritance to them that fear Thy name" (Psalm 60:6 LXX), first printed with its verses at Vespers on the Fourth Sunday of Lent.
[^1168]: Monday of the Sixth Week of Lent initiates the solemn cycle of Palm Week (Седми́ца ва́їй / Ἑβδομὰς τῶν βαΐων), directing the Church's contemplation toward the raising of the four-days-dead Lazarus in Bethany and the triumphal entry of the Lord into Jerusalem.
[^1169]: Sessional hymn following the third stichology of the Psalter on Monday of Palm Week. The hymn marks the formal liturgical entry into the Sixth Week of the Great Fast (Седми́ца ва́їй / Ἑβдоμὰς τῶν βαΐων), interweaving the ascetic completion of the Forty Days with the approaching resurrection of Lazarus and the triumphal entry of the Lord into Jerusalem.
[^1170]: The two Matins canons (triodia) of Monday of Palm Week: the first triodion (Odes 1, 8, and 9) in Tone 1 is by St. Joseph the Hymnographer, emphasizing personal compunction, repentance from spiritual sloth, and liberation from the fate of the unmerciful rich man. The second triodion in Tone 1 is by St. Theodore the Studite, dramatizing the impending journey of Christ from beyond Jordan toward Bethany and Bethphage to raise Lazarus.
[^1171]: Ode 8 of the Studite triodion addresses Bethany directly as the honored home of Lazarus, Martha, and Mary, portraying the village as the holy threshold of the Lord's victory over hades and death.
[^1172]: The Aposticha Idiomelon of Monday Matins in Tone 5 takes up the Gospel archetype of the Rich Man and Poor Lazarus (Luke 16:19–31), invoking the imagery of the beggar cast before the gate to plead for drops of divine mercy before the dread tribunal.
[^1173]: The Sixth Hour of Monday of Palm Week appoints the Troparion of the Prophecy in Tone 6 ("This is, O God, the fearful day..."), accompanied by two prokeimena from the Psalter (Psalms 104 and 105 LXX) and the prophetic reading from Isaiah 48:17–49:4, which speaks of the redemption of Jacob and introduces the Servant Song of the Messiah called from the maternal womb.
[^1174]: Monday of Palm Week at Vespers: the stichera at "Lord, I have cried" combine St. Joseph's hymn of penitential healing on the bed of slothfulness with St. Theodore's contemplation of Christ walking beyond the Jordan and announcing that the sickness of Lazarus is not unto death, but for the glory of God (John 11:4).
[^1175]: The Old Testament reading at Vespers from Genesis 27:1–41 recounts the patriarchal narrative of Isaac blessing Jacob with the dew of heaven and the fatness of the earth, prefiguring the spiritual inheritance granted to the Church from among the Gentiles.
[^1176]: The second reading at Vespers from Proverbs 19:16–25 provides moral exhortations on keeping the divine commandments, showing mercy to the poor as lending unto the Lord, and heeding parental instruction.
[^1177]: Epigraphic defect on physical leaf p427 (printed folio 423) in the concluding Aposticha Theotokion at Monday Vespers. Due to an inking void / printing defect in the Stauropegion edition, words between the left and right fragments are lost: '...го̀ Бг҃а, ро́ждшаѧ всѣ́хъ Творца̀, да всѝ' and 'зове́... хъ на́шихъ.' Restored according to the standard Byzantine-Slavonic Triodion witness: '[И҆зба́ви на́съ ѿ нꙋ́ждъ на́шихъ, Мт҃и Хрⷭ҇та̀] го̀ Бг҃а, ро́ждшаѧ всѣ́хъ Творца̀, да всѝ зове́[мъ тѝ: ра́дꙋйсѧ є҆ди́но предста́тельство дꙋ́ш]хъ на́шихъ.'
[^1178]: Tuesday of Palm Week at Matins: the service continues the prefestal contemplation of Lazarus's sickness and death, appointing penitential sessional hymns from the Octoechos and proper sessional hymns by St. Joseph and St. Theodore.
[^1179]: Appointment of Ode 2 on Tuesday of Palm Week. Outside of the Great Fast, the second biblical ode (the Song of Moses in Deuteronomy 32) is omitted from the daily Matins canon. In the Triodion, Tuesdays retain Ode 2, here paired with triodia by St. Joseph (Tone 6) and St. Theodore (Tone 5).
[^1180]: Ode 9 of Tuesday Matins portrays Lazarus on the brink of physical death ('being about to die tomorrow') and Bethany preparing his tomb, setting the dramatic timeline leading up to Lazarus Saturday.
[^1181]: The Matins Aposticha Idiomelon of Tuesday of Palm Week in Tone 5 combines two biblical warnings against spiritual sloth: Lot fleeing the destruction of Sodom and Gomorrah without looking back (Gen. 19:17, 26), and the parable of Dives and Lazarus (Luke 16:19–31), wherein the beggar is received into Abraham's bosom while the unmerciful rich man burns in torment.
[^1182]: The Sixth Hour on Tuesday of Palm Week appoints the Troparion of the Prophecy in Tone 5 ("An invincible rampart hast Thou given unto our city..."), followed by two prokeimena from the Psalter (Psalms 108 and 109 LXX) and the prophetic reading from Isaiah 49:6–10.
[^1183]: Reading from the Prophecy of Isaiah (49:6–10) at the Sixth Hour of Tuesday: the passage continues the Servant Songs of the Messiah, declaring Christ to be given as a covenant for the people and a light to the Gentiles, establishing the earth and liberating prisoners from spiritual bonds.
[^1184]: The Tuesday Vespers stichera at "Lord, I have cried" in Tone 2 by St. Joseph the Hymnographer allegorize the death of Lazarus into the interior state of the sinner, whose heart lies buried in the tomb of despondency and covered with the stone of insensibility, crying for Christ to awaken it by the Wood of the Cross.
[^1185]: Sticheron by St. Theodore the Studite at Tuesday Vespers in Tone 3 marking the dramatic progression of Palm Week: "Today Lazarus hath breathed his last, and Bethany weeps for him" (Днесь издыше Лазарь), anticipating the Lord's arrival to raise him on Lazarus Saturday.
[^1186]: Reading from Genesis 31:3–16 at Tuesday Vespers: Jacob receives the divine command to depart from Padan-aram and return to Canaan with Leah, Rachel, and his flocks, recounting God's protective intervention against Laban's deceit.
[^1187]: Reading from Proverbs 21:3–21 appointed across Tuesday Vespers (fol. 429–430 / physical leaves p433–p434), contrasting the blessings of righteousness, mercy, and diligent labor with the destruction awaiting deceit and pride.
[^1188]: The Tuesday Vespers Aposticha Idiomelon in Tone 1 contrasts the royal purple robe of dominion and the scarlet mantle of incorruption bestowed upon the soul with the purple robe of mockery placed upon Christ before His crucifixion (Matt. 27:28), calling the Christian to spiritual poverty.
[^1189]: Wednesday of Palm Week at Matins: following the second stichology of the Psalter, the sessional hymn by St. Joseph the Hymnographer in Tone 2 portrays the sinner weighed down by sins as with a grave stone, pleading for Christ to raise him from the tomb of negligence.
[^1190]: Sessional hymn by St. Theodore the Studite following the third stichology on Wednesday Matins in Tone 3: "Today Lazarus, having died, is buried" (Днесь Лазарь умерый погребается), tracking the chronological sequence of the four days in the tomb.
[^1191]: The two Matins canons (triodia) of Wednesday of Palm Week: the first triodion (Odes 3, 8, and 9) in Tone 2 is by St. Joseph the Hymnographer, focused on awakening the mind from the slumber of despondency. The second triodion in Tone 3 is by St. Theodore the Studite, dramatizing Christ's announcement to the disciples that Lazarus is fallen asleep and St. Thomas's courageous resolve to accompany Him into Judea (John 11:16).
[^1192]: Ode 8 of the Studite triodion on Wednesday Matins portrays Lazarus being carried out to the grave ("Today Lazarus, having died, is carried out for burial") while Christ approaches with joy to reveal His divine power as the Life of all.
[^1193]: Ode 9 of the Studite triodion depicts Mary and Martha weeping under the bitter stone of the sepulcher, while Christ declares from afar that He rejoices for the apostles' sakes that He was not there, in order that they may believe (John 11:15).
[^1194]: The Matins Aposticha Idiomelon of Wednesday of Palm Week in Tone 1 offers an allegorical and patristic exposition of the Parable of Dives and Lazarus (Luke 16:19–31): Israel, formerly robed in priestly and royal vesture under the Law and the Prophets, now thirsts outside the Kingdom for having rejected the crucified Christ, while the Gentiles, formerly destitute of truth, are now comforted in Abraham's bosom, clothed in the purple of Christ's blood and the scarlet of baptism.
[^1195]: The Sixth Hour on Wednesday of Palm Week appoints the Troparion of the Prophecy in Tone 5 ("Deal Thy mercies with us, O Lord..."), accompanied by two prokeimena from the Psalter (Psalms 112 and 113 LXX) and the prophetic reading from Isaiah 58:1–11.
[^1196]: Reading from the Prophecy of Isaiah (58:1–11) at the Sixth Hour of Wednesday: the definitive prophetic charter on the true fast, rejecting empty ascetic posturing accompanied by contention and oppression, and demanding active works of mercy, the liberation of the bound, and the feeding of the hungry.
[^1197]: Wednesday of Palm Week at Vespers: the idiomelon at "Lord, I have cried" in Tone 5 resumes the theme of Dives and Lazarus, imploring Christ to grant the speaker the poverty in sins of Lazarus (Лазара мя сотвори, нищаго грѣхами) rather than the fiery torment of the heartless rich man.
[^1198]: The prosomoia stichera by St. Theodore the Studite at Wednesday Vespers in Tone 5 track Christ's physical journey from beyond the Jordan towards Bethany, announcing to the apostles that Lazarus is dead and that He goes to conquer death.
[^1199]: St. Theodore's sticheron in Tone 6 marks the second day of Lazarus in the grave: "Lazarus passes his second day in the tomb" (Двоедневствуетъ Лазарь во гробѣ), describing his vision of the departed from all ages held in the bonds of hades.
[^1200]: Reading from Genesis 43:26–31 and 45:1 at Wednesday Vespers, prefiguring the revelation of Christ unto His brethren. The heading in the Stauropegion edition contains an epigraphic misprint, reading Chapter 33 ("Глава̀ л҃г") instead of Chapter 43 ("м҃г").
[^1201]: The conclusion of the reading from Genesis (45:1–16) at Wednesday Vespers recounts the dramatic self-revelation of Joseph to his brethren in Egypt and his invitation for Jacob and his entire household to dwell in Goshen.
[^1202]: Psalm 115:5, 1 LXX (116:14, 10 MT), appointed as the second prokimenon of Wednesday Vespers of Palm Week, celebrating thanksgiving and redemption.
[^1203]: Reading from Proverbs 21:23–31 and 22:1–4 at Wednesday Vespers, contrasting divine wisdom, upright speech, and true riches in the fear of the Lord with arrogance and sloth.
[^1204]: Sessional hymn by St. Joseph the Hymnographer following the second stichology at Thursday Matins in Tone 5, urging the faithful to yoke contemplation and action in preparation to greet Christ.
[^1205]: Sessional hymn by St. Theodore the Studite following the third stichology at Thursday Matins in Tone 6, marking Lazarus's second day in the grave.
[^1206]: Incipit of the Heirmos of Ode 4 of the first Matins canon (triodion) by St. Joseph the Hymnographer: "Comprehending Thy divine self-emptying, O Christ..." (Hab. 3:2).
[^1207]: Heirmos of Ode 4 of the second Matins canon (triodion) by St. Theodore the Studite in Tone 6: "I have heard Thy report and was afraid..." (Hab. 3:2).
[^1208]: Incipit of the Heirmos of Ode 8 of the first Matins canon by St. Joseph the Hymnographer: "Unto Thee, the Creator of all..."
[^1209]: Incipit of the Heirmos of Ode 8 of the second Matins canon by St. Theodore the Studite: "Thy venerable Children in the furnace..."
[^1210]: Incipit of the Heirmos of Ode 9 of the first Matins canon by St. Joseph the Hymnographer: "Rejoice, O Isaiah, a Virgin has conceived in womb..."
[^1211]: Incipit of the Heirmos of Ode 9 of the second Matins canon by St. Theodore the Studite: "The ineffable birthgiving of the seedless conception..."
[^1212]: The Matins Aposticha Idiomelon of Thursday of Palm Week in Tone 5 combines allusions to Abraham entertaining the angels (Gen. 18:1–8; Heb. 13:2) with the Parable of the Sheep and the Goats (Matt. 25:31–46), calling for fasting and almsgiving in order to be placed on Christ's right hand.
[^1213]: Theotokion at the Matins Aposticha on Thursday of Palm Week, modeled after the similar Marian hymns of supplication.
[^1214]: The Sixth Hour on Thursday of Palm Week appoints the Troparion of the Prophecy in Tone 1, with prokeimena from Psalms 117 and 118 LXX and the reading from Isaiah 65:8–16.
[^1215]: Psalm 117:20, 1 LXX (118:20, 1 MT), chanted before the Sixth Hour prophecy, foreshadowing Christ's triumphal entry through the gates of Jerusalem on Palm Sunday.
[^1216]: Reading from the Prophecy of Isaiah (65:8–16) at the Sixth Hour of Thursday: the Lord promises the preservation of a faithful remnant while condemning idolaters who set tables for demons, declaring that His servants shall eat, drink, and rejoice under a new name.
[^1217]: Psalm 118:32, 33 LXX (119:32, 33 MT), chanted as the second prokimenon of the Sixth Hour on Thursday of Palm Week.
[^1218]: Stichera prosomoia by St. Joseph the Hymnographer at Thursday Vespers in Tone 4, depicting the two disciples sent by Christ to find the colt (Matt. 21:1–7; Mark 11:1–7).
[^1219]: Sticheron prosomoion by St. Theodore the Studite at Thursday Vespers in Tone 8, contrasting Christ's celestial enthronement upon the Seraphim with His humble entry into Jerusalem upon a foal.
[^1220]: Psalm 119:1, 2 LXX (120:1, 2 MT), the first Gradual Psalm (Song of Ascents), appointed as the first prokimenon of Thursday Vespers of Palm Week.
[^1221]: Reading from Genesis 46:1–7 appointed across Thursday Vespers (fol. 443–444 / physical leaves p447–p448): God encourages Jacob at Beersheba ("the well of the oath"), promising to accompany him into Egypt and multiply his descendants.
[^1222]: Psalm 120:7, 1 LXX (121:7, 1 MT), the second Gradual Psalm, appointed as the second prokimenon of Thursday Vespers of Palm Week.
[^1223]: Reading from Proverbs 23:15–35 and 24:1–5 at Thursday Vespers, warning against drunkenness, lust, and evil companions while praising filial piety and divine wisdom.
[^1224]: The Thursday Vespers Aposticha Idiomelon in Tone 8 resumes the central Lenten and Palm Week typology of the Parable of Dives and Lazarus (Luke 16:19–31), praying for deliverance from the hard-heartedness of the rich man.
[^1225]: Cross-Theotokion at Thursday Vespers Aposticha, contemplating the grief of the Mother of God at the crucifixion of her Son.
[^1226]: Sessional hymn by St. Joseph the Hymnographer after the second stichology at Friday Matins in Tone 4, meditating on the sending of the two disciples to fetch the colt.
[^1227]: Sessional hymn by St. Theodore the Studite after the third stichology at Friday Matins in Tone 8, anticipating the overthrow of death by the raising of Lazarus.
[^1228]: Incipit of the Heirmos of Ode 5 of the first Matins canon by St. Joseph the Hymnographer: "Bestow light upon me, O Lord..."
[^1229]: Heirmos of Ode 5 of the second Matins canon by St. Theodore the Studite in Tone 8: "Dispel the gloom of my soul, O Giver of Light..."
[^1230]: Incipit of the Heirmos of Ode 8 of the first Matins canon by St. Joseph the Hymnographer: "The earth and all that is therein, bless the Lord..."
[^1231]: Incipit of the Heirmos of Ode 8 of the second Matins canon by St. Theodore the Studite in Tone 8: "The Angels and the heavens, bless Him Who sits upon the throne of glory..."
[^1232]: Incipit of the Heirmos of Ode 9 of the first Matins canon by St. Joseph the Hymnographer: "For He that is mighty hath done to me great things..." (Luke 1:49).
[^1233]: Incipit of the Heirmos of Ode 9 of the second Matins canon by St. Theodore the Studite in Tone 8: "That which was prefigured on the mountain unto the lawgiver in the fire and the bush..."
[^1234]: Rubric directing the singing of the Exaposteilarion (Exaposteilarion) corresponding to the Tone of the week.
[^1235]: Composed by Byzantine Emperor Leo VI the Wise (866–912), an ascetic idiomelon on spiritual vigilance and the bridal chamber of Christ.
[^1236]: The classic concluding Doxasticon of the Great Forty Days ("Душеполезную совершивше Четыредесятницу"), marking the theological completion of Lent before the services of Lazarus Saturday and Passion Week.
[^1237]: Psalm 121:9, 1 (LXX) [Psalm 122:9, 1 Masoretic].
[^1238]: Isaiah 66:10–19 (LXX).
[^1239]: Isaiah 66:19–24 (LXX).
[^1240]: Psalm 122:3, 1 (LXX) [Psalm 123:3, 1 Masoretic].
[^1241]: Colophon rubric marking the formal completion of the Great Fast (Forty Days) at the Sixth Hour on Friday of the Sixth Week (Palm Week). In the Ruthenian tradition, this boundary separates the liturgical Forty Days from the Appendix and the subsequent services of Lazarus Saturday, Palm Sunday, and Holy Week.
